SMALL BUSINESS ADMINISTRATION WASHINGTON, D.C. 20416 OFFICE OF THE ADMINISTRATOR THE PRESIDENT The White House Washington, D.C. 

MY DEAR MR. PRESIDENT: 

July 9, 1964

Pursuant to the provisions of the Small Business Act, as amended, I have the honor to submit to you this report on the service the Small Business Administration rendered to America's small business concerns in 1963. 

In several instances the report refers to new SBA programs, or revisions of existing programs, that were being readied for adoption at year's end. These programs or changes have been in effect for several months now, and are proving of substantial value to small firms. In addition, the SBA has taken other actions which have been highly beneficial to small business. Because of your concern for the welfare of the Nation's 

4.6 million small businesses, I know you will be interested also in a brief report on these more recent and significant SBA actions: 

Revised Small Loan Program.-In January 1964 the SBA adopted a revised and simplified small loan program to help with the financing requirements of very small retail, service, and other firms. The need for such a program has been clearly demonstrated. In the first 4 months of operation of the program, these small loans-up to $15,000 and for a maximum period of 6 years-made up about one-fourth of all business loans approved by the SBA.

Minority Groups' Program.—Early this year, the SBA began a pilot loan and management training program for very small businesses of Philadelphia, with particular emphasis on helping Negro businessmen obtain needed, but previously unavailable, financing. A similar program subsequently was placed in effect in New York City. 

The response in both cities-by businessmen and civic and community leaders-has been highly gratifying. Since the program promises to create additional jobs and income in areas which have long suffered from poverty, the SBA plans to extend the program to areas of unemployment in other major cities as well. 

Small Business Investment Companies.-The confidence of, and in, the SBIC industry has been greatly strengthened in recent months as a result of SBA and other Government actions which indicate firm support of the SBIC program. These actions include congressional amendments to the Small Business Investment Act that substantially benefit SBIC's; liberalization of SBA regulations governing the industry; creation by SBA of an Industry-Government Liaison Committee to help solve problems of the industry; acceptance by the Internal Revenue Service of a 10-percent SBIC loss reserve for bad debts, and adoption by the Securities and Exchange Commission of new rules that facilitate operations of publicly held SBIC's. 

Community-Area Development.-The SBA has sharply stepped up its work in promoting the economic growth of underdeveloped communities and areas. The agency is participating more actively in the overall area redevelopment program, and in programs to stimulate the economic growth of rural areas and the Appalachian region. 1131518 iii

The SBA also contributed substantially to preparation of the President's Appalachian Regional Commission Report and related legislation submitted to the Congress. 

Small Business Management.-The SBA has called on retired business executives to make available their experience and talent for counseling of small firms. More than 

2,400 retired executives already have responded to the call and registered with the agency. Their guidance should be tremendously helpful to small business, and especially to those businessmen who are just entering the field. 

Those, briefly, are a few of the major steps the SBA has taken thus far in 1964 to make its services more readily available to small business concerns, and more responsive to their needs. 

I am hopeful and confident that these SBA actions, as well as others now being developed, will enable the agency to be of even greater assistance to small business during the current year than it was in 1963. 

Identical letters sent to- 

The President of the Senate, The Speaker of the House of Representatives. 

Respectfully yours,

Вдови 

EUGENE P. FOLEY, Administrator iv

Section I Highlights of 1963 

The small business community as a whole benefited substantially from continued expansion of the Nation's economy in 1963. At the same time, small business was an important contributor to growth of the overall economy which, in 1963, 

• A rise in the gross national product to $585 billion from $555 billion the previous year; • An increase to 4.86 million in the Nation's business population, of which 4.6 million are small concerns; • A decline for the second successive year in the rate of business failures to the lowest level since 1959; • An increase for the third consecutive year in the income of small unincorporated enterprises; • A record annual rate of more than $40 billion of expenditures for new plant and equipment; • The largest work force in the Nation's history, more than 70 million employed; • New record highs in consumer income and consumer spending, and • A record level in corporate profits after taxes. 

How SBA's Programs Helped Small Firms 

Some of SBA's programs were expanded and adjusted during 1963-and other programs added to make the agency's services more helpful to small businesses. Highlights of the agency's activities included: 

Minority Groups' Program: For the first time in its history, SBA instituted in 1963 a program for minority groups to insure them every opportunity to receive full assistance under the agency's programs and services. A special Assistant to the Administrator was named to head the program and charged specifically with informing minority groups of the availability of SBA's services. In addition to implementing this program and the SBA equality policy, the Special 

Assistant for Minority Groups represents SBA 

on the President's Committee on Equal Employ ment Opportunity and on the White House Sub cabinet Group on Civil Rights. At year's end a pilot project was readied for low-income areas of Philadelphia County whereby very small businesses could receive loans up to $6,000 for 6 years at 4 percent interest. If successful, it will be applied to other neighborhoods of high unemployment.

Small business investment companies: The 

SBA licensed 65 additional small business investment companies with total capitalization of $22.3 million to provide long-term loans and equity capital to small firms. At the year's end, 16 licenses had been canceled, and the 692 SBIC's in operation were based in 42 States, the District of Colum bia, Guam, Puerto Rico, and the Virgin Islands. 

These SBIC's had $595,747,804 total capital for use in small business loans and investments at the close of 1963. This included $117,873,683 of SBA funds or a ratio of 4 to 1 of private funds versus Government funds invested in the program. 

Community-area development: Loans to local development companies totaled 115 for $18.2 million and provided more than 6,000 new job opportunities. In 1962 SBA approved 100 loans for $14.1 million. Approximately 30 percent of the 

115 approved loans were for the distressed Appalachian area. The agency cooperated with the 

Area Redevelopment Administration, other Federal agencies, and State and local authorities to help communities establish or expand job-creating industrial and commercial projects. During 1963, SBA completed processing loans totaling $92,- 015,878 for 198 redevelopment projects approved by the ARA-nearly double the number and almost four times the dollar value of such loans processed by SBA in 1962.

Business loans: Important developments in the financial activities of the agency included con- 1

version of SBA's Deferred Bank Participation 

Program into a Loan Guaranty Plan and a reduction in SBA service fees for immediate participation loans made and serviced by banks. The agency also developed a Revised Small Loan Program for the smaller firms, and a loan program for very small retail and service establishments of minority groups. SBA approved nearly 5,000 bank-SBA and direct SBA loans for about $300 million. Banks participated with SBA in more than 65 percent of all business loans approved by the agency.

Disaster loans: The SBA approved 2,503 loans for $30.1 million to aid victims of storms and similar natural disasters in restoring or replacing homes, businesses, and other property, or to assist small firms that suffered economic injury from drought or excessive rainfall, or through displacement by federally aided construction projects. In 

1963, the agency received 2,919 disaster loan applications.

Government contracts: A record total of $2.4 billion of proposed Government purchases were set aside for competitive bidding by small firms through the joint action of SBA and purchasing agencies. These set-asides resulted in award to small firms of 48,664 contracts valued at $1.9 billion. In 1962 SBA and purchasing agencies jointly reserved for small business proposed purchases valued at $2.2 billion, resulting in 54,179 contracts totaling $1.8 billion. In fiscal 1963 contract awards to small firms amounted to 16.8 percent of the total dollar value of all Government contracts and 15.8 percent of the value of military contracts; the respective figures for the prior fiscal year were 19.3 and 17.7 percent.

Small business management: The continued upswing in the Nation's economy brought new changes in urban and rural markets, development of new products, and new methods of production and distribution. To help small firms adjust to and profit from these changes, SBA broadened its management assistance to small firms. During 1963, SBA introduced a new 1-day "workshop" program for persons planning to start their own businesses. The agency also broadened its programs of management counseling, publications, courses, conferences, and research. 

Improved operations and administration:

Organizational realinements and functional changes, designed to improve SBA's programs and to increase agency efficiency, were put into effect last year. Major changes included creation of the positions of Executive Administrator and 

Special Assistant for Minority Groups, and estab lishment of an Office of Audits. Other changes included consolidation of all loan appraisal functions under the Deputy Administrator for financial Assistance, and creation of an Office of Assist ant Administrator for Administration. During the year SBA developed a standardized plan for field office processing of loan applications; placed major emphasis on training top and middle managers; standardized and reduced the number of delegations of authority; assigned resident auditors to each of the agency's regional offices; and established a unit in the Office of the General Counsel to coordinate interagency programs on behalf of small business.

It is too soon to determine the full effect of these changes and actions. However, it already is evident that they have contributed to increased agency efficiency and economy and greater service to small business. 2

Section II The Economy and Small Business 

By nearly all measures, the performance of the Nation's economy in 1963 was outstanding and small business contributed to and shared in the economic gains. The economic progress of small firms was reflected in these figures: 

1. The business population increased by about 

45,000 small businesses. Since the low point of the recession in early 1961, the population has grown by approximately 130,000, to 4,860,000, of which about 4,600,000 are small firms. 

2. Business failures, which occur primarily among small firms, declined for the second straight year and were the smallest in number since 1959. 

3. Income of small unincorporated enterprises increased for the third consecutive year. 

4. Sales of smaller manufacturing corporations in the first three quarters of the year rose by 8 per- 'cent, compared with a 5-percent gain for the larger corporations. 

The economic stimulus provided by the President's tax reduction measure should assure a high level of economic activity this year, and help small firms match or exceed the good year they enjoyed in 1963. The Council of Economic Advisers has said that the tax reductions should result in a 6.5percent increase in the gross national product to a range of $618 to $628 billion. 

Small Businesses 

Up to 4.6 Million

At the start of 1964, the Nation's business population was estimated by the U.S. Department of 

Commerce at 4,860,000, of which about 4,600,000 were small businesses.

The Department estimates that 430,000 new businesses were started in 1962 (1963 figures not available). However, more than 387,000 concerns discontinued operations the same year because of failure, unsatisfactory earnings, merger or acquisition, illness or retirement of owners, or other reasons. More than one-half the net increase of 

43,000 firms was accounted for by businesses which supply services.

In recent years, firms which provide services have profited from increased personal consumption expenditures made possible by higher individual incomes. Personal consumption expenditures for services rose from $56.9 billion (22 percent of the gross national product) in 1948 to $154.5 billion (26 percent of the gross national product) in 1963. As a result, businesses which provide services increased in number during this period from 728,000 to 942,000.

Business Incorporations 

Increase Slightly

New business incorporations totaled 185,622 last year, compared with 182,057 in 1962. During the second half of 1963, new incorporations were 6 percent higher than in the same period of 1962. 

Passage of the Technical Amendments Act of 

1958, which permits small corporations to be taxed as partnerships, has made the corporate form of organization more popular. In 1953 the number of new business incorporations was 29 percent of the total number of businesses formed. By 1962 the number had risen to 42 percent. 

An analysis of new business incorporations by geographic divisions shows that the increase in 1963 occurred in six of the nine geographic divi sions: East North Central, West North Central, East South Central, West South Central, Moun tain, and Pacific areas. Incorporations in these areas increased from 90,061 in 1962 to 94,267 in 1963, a gain of 5 percent. The three other regions-New England, Middle Atlantic, and South Atlantic-showed a decline of less than 1 percent, from 91,996 to 91,355.

Since 1956 the West North Central States (those 

States immediately west of the Mississippi River), the Mountain States, and the South Central States have accounted for an increasing share of incorporations. New business incorporations in the

other five regions also increased in this period, but in each they represented a smaller part of the total than in 1956. However, the heavily populated and industrialized Middle Atlantic States continued to account for the substantial portion of all new business incorporations and in 1963 represented 27 percent of the total. In order of importance, other geographic areas with substantial shares of 1963 incorporations were the South 

Atlantic, East North Central, and Pacific Regions. 

Failures in 1963 

Lowest Since 1959

Because of the continuing rise in economic activity, business failures declined in 1963 for the second straight year, and were the smallest in number since 1959. 1956..

15,782, and 16 percent below the postwar peak of 

17,075 in 1961. 80 55 60 75 50 40 45 The failure rate per 10,000 firms included in the 

Dun & Bradstreet Reference Book was 56 last year. In 1962 the rate was 61, while in 1961 it was 64.

The figures on business failures include bankruptcies and other arrangements involving a loss to creditors. As such they represent a small fraction of 1 percent of the total business population. 

However, failure figures do not include business discontinuances because of losses or poor earnings.

Although precise figures on these discontinuances are not available, studies indicate there are considerably more than 100,000 each year. 11 22220 25 10 10 10 10

Business failures last year were lower than the 1961 and 1962 levels in all major categories except commercial services. Here, there was a 3-percent increase from 1962 to 1963. Commercial services include passenger and freight transportation; personal services such as cleaning, and business and repair services, such as television repairs. 

The largest percentage decrease in failures last year, 12 percent, occurred in the retail trades. 

Trades which showed the greatest decrease included food and liquor, general merchandise, furniture and homefurnishings, and lumber, building materials and hardware businesses. 

Because of a high level of construction activity, firms in that field were more stable in 1963 than in the previous 2 years. Last year they were second only to the retail trades in decline in failures.

Failures involving liabilities of less than $100,000 were relatively fewer in number in 1963 than in 1962. However, there was an increase in failures involving liabilities of $100,000 or more. 

A Dun & Bradstreet study of the 15,782 failures in 1962 found that 45 percent of them resulted from incompetent management. Another 46 percent were attributable to either a lack of managerial or line (product or service) experience or of well-balanced experience in all necessary phases of operations. These findings illustrate the need for continuation and expansion of management counseling and technical assistance to small firms, and particularly new ones. If assistance could be made widely available to new or prospective small businesses, many failures and much economic waste could be prevented. 

Unincorporated Enterprises 

Again Gain in Income

third consecutive year. The higher level of economic activity-especially the higher rate of personal consumption expenditures-lifted the income of most unincorporated enterprises. Their 1963 income is estimated at $37.2 billion, 3 percent higher than the 1962 income of $36.0 billion. 

The distribution of income by industry divisions is not available for 1963. From 1961 to 1962, however, all groupings of industry divisions showed increases. 

The manufacturing industries showed an increase in income in 1962 of almost 8 percent. On the other hand, the largest absolute increases occurred in industry divisions made up largely of small concerns the services and the combined retail and wholesale trades. Income in these two principal categories increased by about 4 percent each and in 1962 made up over 70 percent of the total income of unincorporated enterprises.

In recent years the proportion of income accounted for by services has tended to increase, while retail and wholesale income has declined slightly. In 1962 the service trades' income represented 37 percent of the total, while retail and wholesale represented 34 percent.

Manufacturing Corporations' 

Sales, Earnings Increase

The changing position of smaller manufacturers is indicated by data on sales and earnings of manufacturing corporations published by the Federal 

Trade Commission and the Securities and Ex change Commission. 

Sales. During the first three quarters of 1963, sales of all manufacturing corporations (except newspapers) amounted to $304,665 million-a 6percent increase over sales of $288,209 million in the same period of 1962. Sales of smaller corporations (those with assets under $1 million) were up relatively more, from $37,896 million to $41,004 million-an increase of 8 percent. Sales of the larger manufacturing corporations (those with assets over $1 million) rose 5 percent, from $250,313 million to $263,661 million. 

Before-Taxes Earnings. Earnings before taxes of smaller manufacturing corporations in the first three quarters of 1963 increased to $1,375 million from $1,336 million in the comparable period of 1962, a gain of 3 percent. Included in these tables were higher third-quarter 1963 earnings of $584 million as compared with third-quarter 1962 earnings of $533 million. On the other hand, earnings before taxes of larger corporations rose to $23,973 million from $22,204 million in the first three quarters of 1962, an increase of 8 percent. Included in the totals for larger corporations were substantially higher third-quarter 1963 earnings of $7,921 million, compared with third-quarter 

1962 earnings of $7,062 million. 

After-Taxes Earnings. Earnings after taxes of all manufacturing corporations in the first three quarters of 1963 increased to $14,022 million from $12,882 million in the same period of 1962, a gain of almost 9 percent. 

Earnings after taxes of smaller manufacturing corporations were practically the same as in the comparable period of 1962. Their after-taxes profits in the first three quarters of that year had increased by 81 percent over the comparable period of 1961; however, in the 1963 period there was no increase over 1962. The after-taxes profits of smaller corporations totaled $743 million in the first three quarters of 1963, as compared with $744 million in the comparable period of 1962. Their third-quarter earnings after taxes increased to $337 million in 1963 from $310 million in the same period of 1962.

Larger corporations' earnings after taxes increased in the first three quarters of 1963 to $13,279 million from $12,138 million in the like period of 1962, a gain of 9 percent. Their thirdquarter earnings after taxes increased to $4,448 million from $3,917 million in the same quarter of 1962.

Return on Stockholders' Equity. The equity of stockholders in smaller manufacturing corporations increased from $10,975 million at the end of the third quarter 1962 to $11,710 million at the end of the third quarter 1963, a gain of 7 percent. This compared with 5 percent increase in stockholders' equity in the larger manufacturing corporations, from $171,727 million to $179,519 million. However, during the postwar years the growth of stockholders' equity of smaller manufacturing corporations lagged behind growth of equity in the larger manufacturing corporations.

The rate of return based on stockholders' equity was about the same in 1963 as in 1962. For the smaller manufacturing corporations, the annual rate of return before taxes in the first three quarters of 1963 averaged 16.3 percent, compared with 

16.7 percent in the comparable period of 1962. 

After taxes, the earnings on stockholders equity for the smaller manufacturing corporations decreased from an annual rate of 9.3 percent in the first three quarters of 1962 to a rate of 8.8 percent in the comparable period of 1963. 

For the larger manufacturing corporations, the rate of return before taxes increased slightly, from an annual rate of 17.4 percent in the first three quarters of 1962 to 18 percent in the corresponding period of 1963. After taxes, the annual rate of return in the first three quarters of 1963 was 10 percent, compared with a 9.5-percent return in the same period of 1962. 

More Mergers

Last Year The Federal Trade Commission recorded 1,311 

mergers during 1963, a 4-percent increase over the 

1,260 mergers in 1962 and the highest number recorded by the Federal Trade Commission. A total of 972 of the mergers were mining and manufacturing firms. 10

In some instances, of course, mergers and acquisitions are desirable. This is particularly true where two or three very small businesses merge to form a new business enterprise of sufficient. strength to either maintain or increase their competitive position in the market. On the other hand, a significant number of the mergers and acquisitions recorded by the Federal Trade Commission may be of such size as to weaken the competitive position of small business. 

The following table shows the trend in mergers from 1955 through 1963:

Year: 

19551956 

1960. 1961_ 1962SBA Research Aids Small Firms Number of mergers 846 905 941 899 1,050 1, 012 1, 234 1, 260 1, 311 As part of its efforts to improve the position of small business in our economy, SBA studies matters which materially affect the competitive strength of small business, as well as the effect on small business of Federal laws, programs, and regulations.

Recent agency studies have dealt with tax relief for small business, Government financing of research and development, and dual distribution. 

Tax Relief. The SBA has played an active role in the national administration's program to obtain income tax relief for business enterprises. 

The SBA cooperated with the Treasury Department in formulating the tax reduction and tax reform proposals of the late President John F.

Kennedy, which were particularly significant to small business, and which were included in large part in H.R. 8363.

The SBA also prepared and submitted statements in support of H.R. 8363 to the House Ways and Means Committee and the Senate Finance 

Committee. In these statements, SBA stressed the importance to small business of income tax relief and income tax reform. In particular, SBA urged adoption of those provisions of the bill which would (a) reverse corporate normal and surtax rates; (b) repeal the penalty tax on corporations filing consolidated returns and impose a penalty on multiunit corporations that elect to claim multiple surtax exemptions; (c) increase the dividend exclusion from $50 to $100; (d) liberalize the provisions for capital loss carryovers and income averaging; and (e) restrict opportunities for abusing the sick pay exclusion privilege. 

Some SBA-licensed small business investment companies have experienced or anticipate relatively heavy bad debt losses, because of the inherent risks involved in the types of loans and investments they make. Accordingly, most SBIC's have built up liberal reserves for bad debt losses, and have taken the position that these reserves should be deductible in computing income tax liability. The SBA, as the official spokesman for small business before other governmental agencies, asked the Internal Revenue Service to study the issue. The SBA also provided IRS detailed information on SBIC operations and gave other assistance. The IRS formally announced its decision on the matter in a Technical Information Release of De cember 24, 1963: that it will allow small business investment companies to establish bad debt reserve ceilings equal to 10 percent of their outstanding loans as reasonable reserves under section 166 (c) of the Internal Revenue Code of 1954. 

The 10-percent ceiling is to apply for a period of 10 years beginning with 1959, after which a SBIC's own loss experience will be used to determine the reasonableness of further additions to its reserve. After 1968 a new 

SBIC, or one that has not been in existence a sufficient number of years to provide adequate loss experience data for establishing reasonable bad debt reserves, will be permitted to use an average loss experience factor computed on an industrywide basis until it has sufficient loss experience of its own. 

Complete details concerning the new rule will be issued by IRS at a later date.

Research and Development. In September 

1963 the House of Representatives created a Select Committee on Government Research to investigate thoroughly the many research programs being conducted by the Federal Government. Because of small business' desire and need to participate in Government-financed research and development, SBA is keenly interested in the work of this committee.

The agency, therefore, is preparing for the committee an internal report on the subject, with particular emphasis on small business participation. 

Included will be an identification of various problem areas encountered by small firms which perform or want to perform research and development work-problems which undoubtedly 13

contribute significantly to the minor role now played by small business in the research effort. 

These data are being compiled from SBA-sponsored research studies and other sources. 

Dual Distribution. Dual distribution by man ufacturers is a serious problem for various segments of small business. Pressed by a need to cut costs and expand markets, many integrated manufacturers have broadened their operations to the point that they now compete with their own customers in secondary manufacturing, wholesaling, and retailing.

On December 10, 1963, the SBA Administrator presented SBA's views on dual distribution before 

Subcommittee No. 4 of the House Select Com mittee on Small Business. He emphasized that there is a lack of detailed information about the competitive effects on small business, but pointed out that the basic problems of small firms affected by dual distribution are the cost of obtaining good locations and modern equipment, and the need for skilled management. The Administrator told the subcommittee that SBA would consider the possibility of giving greater help with these management and financial problems. SBA has initiated attempts to foster closer cooperation between itself, the Federal Trade Commission, and the Antitrust Division of the Justice Department to develop additional solutions to the problems of dual distribution. Officials of the agencies are in process of preparing an agenda for a conference of the three agencies in the near future.

Research Contracts. In fiscal year 1963 SBA awarded four contracts for research studies. 

A study of reports on Government-sponsored research and development in the plastics industry is being made by Bjorksten Research Laboratories, Inc., Madison, Wis. This study includes a survey of small businesses primarily engaged in the plastics industry to test the usefulness of descriptions, data sheets, and usages developed from the Government reports. Suggestions will be made for procedures to be followed for similar studies in other industries.

The International Marketing Institute, Cam bridge, Mass., is studying methods and procedures to be followed in developing community exporting programs for smaller firms. The broad objective of this study is to determine the important characteristics of typical exporters. It is hoped that this in turn will make it possible to identify small manufacturing firms which could profitably enter exporting. The manufacturers will then be invited to attend workshops conducted by experts in the field of international marketing. 

A third study, undertaken for SBA by Prof. 

Dudley W. Johnson, Edmonds, Wash., deals with sources of information and data pertaining to the cost of short-term and long-term money for small firms. Professor Johnson's report has set forth some tentative conclusions, and has suggested methods for use in a broader study. 

Another study, being made by S. M. Rubel &

Associates, Chicago, Ill., is designed to provide 

SBA the most feasible method of determining whether the number of small business investment companies in a given geographic area has reached the "saturation point," and licensing, therefore, should be discontinued. 14

Section III Financing Small Firms 

A continuing problem of small business is difficulty in obtaining adequate financing on reason able terms. The Small Business Administration helps solve this problem through counseling on financial matters, assistance in obtaining private financing, loans in participation with banks, and direct Government loans.

During the year SBA approved nearly 5,000 loans to help small firms strengthen their operations, expand and modernize their facilities, and increase job opportunities and payrolls in their communities. About 65 percent of these loans were made in participation with local banks. 

Important developments in the financial activities of the agency in 1963 included conversion of 

SBA's Deferred Bank Participation Program into a Loan Guaranty Plan, and a reduction in SBA service fees for immediate participation loans. 

This was done to increase participation by private lenders in SBA loans and to reduce the Government's outlay of funds.

The agency also developed a Revised Small Loan Program for the smaller firms affording liberalized and simplified assistance, and a loan program for very small retail and service establishments of minority groups in neighborhoods of chronic unemployment in furtherance of the President's war on poverty. (For details, see sec. IX.) 

Business Loan Applications and Approvals

During January-December 1963 SBA received 

10,004 loan applications and approved 4975 loans totaling $278,202,000. Only in calendar years 

1961 and 1962 did SBA exceed this total. 

Through December 31, 1963, SBA had approved a cumulative total of 40,047 business loans for $2,015,089,690.

SBA Financial Counseling Aids Small Firm Owners and Managers 

During January-December, SBA counseled 14,000 small businessmen on specific financial problems.

In counseling with a small businessman, SBA's financial specialists seek to pinpoint his problems and recommend possible solutions. Often the problem is resolved without need of borrowing. 

However, if borrowing appears to be the answer,

SBA helps to obtain the needed financing from private lending institutions. Frequently, SBA participates in loans with private sources. If none of the needed funds can be obtained from private sources, SBA will consider a direct Government loan to the small business. 

However, the agency's program of financial assistance is designed to supplement the lending activities of commercial banks, not to compete with them. Therefore, SBA always strives for bank participation, and one of the agency's principal objectives is increased cooperation with banks in meeting the term credit needs of small businesses.

A new loan plan, discussed in the following section and introduced in the last half of 1963, should help to achieve this objective. 15

New Loan Plan Aims for Greater Bank Participation 

The SBA and the American Bankers Association now are making a cooperative effort to provide qualified small businesses with term loans from their primary source of credit, commercial banks. To further this program, SBA introduced on July 1, 1963, a Loan Guaranty Plan in lieu of the agency's Deferred Bank Participation Plan.

This was one of the most important developments in the financial assistance activities of SBA in 1963. Under the Loan Guaranty Plan, the bank and SBA enter into a written guaranty agreement whereby the loan is disbursed and serviced by the bank, with SBA guaranteeing repayment of not more than 90 percent of the unpaid principal and interest of the loan.

To encourage increased participation by private lenders in SBA loans and to reduce the Government's outlay of funds, SBA has changed the service fee for immediate participation loans made and serviced by the participating institutions from 

1/2 to 1 percent per annum regardless of the amount of the bank's participation to ½ of 1 percent where the bank participates for at least 

25 percent of a loan; and 1/4 of 1 percent where the bank's participation is less than 25 percent. 

SBA also developed a Revised Small Loan Program scheduled to be introduced during January 1964. This revised program, geared to the needs of smaller firms which are seeking loans of $15,000 or less, is designed to simplify and liberalize, to the extent possible, requirements for such loans, made either in participation with banks or directly by SBA. It is anticipated that the formal announcement of this program will stimulate both the inquiry and application workload. 

Banks Participate in More Than 

65 Percent of All Business Loans 

During 1963 commercial banks participated with SBA in 3,240 loans to small firms, representing approximately 65.1 percent of all business loans approved by the agency during the year. 

Participation loans accounted for $216,005,000, or 

77.6 percent of the total dollar amount of loan. approvals this period.

Maintenance of the high degree of bank participation during 1963 is particularly gratifying because SBA feels that lending to businesses preferably should be carried out at the local level in close cooperation with local banks. All SBA loan applications are accepted and processed in the agency's field offices. These offices have been given broadened authority to approve business loans, so that relatively few applications are now sent to Washington for final action.

Agency Makes Loans of 

Interest to Other Agencies

As a part of its overall business loan program, SBA cooperates with other Federal agencies by providing financial assistance to small firms whose activities are of particular interest to those agencies. For example, SBA makes loans to hospitals and convalescent and nursing homes which are privately owned and operated for profit; finances the construction of aircraft storage hangars for rental purposes; and makes loans for access road construction to small firms which purchase timber from national forests. 

Agency Has Special Plan for 

"Pools" of Small Firms 

A special plan is available to finance "pools" or corporations formed and capitalized by groups of small businesses. By law, SBA may lend such a pool of small firms as much as $250,000 multiplied by the number of small firms participating in the pool. The borrowing corporation may use the loan to obtain for the use of pool members raw materials, supplies, equipment, and inventories, or the benefits of research and development, or to establish facilities for such purpose. 

SBA Strives for More 

Effective Aid to Borrowers

The agency's responsibility for assisting small firms does not end when a loan is disbursed. The 

SBA also seeks to insure that the financial help it gives small firms is effective. 

Therefore, loans not serviced by participating banks are administered by agency field offices in areas in which the borrowers are located. 

Through continuing review of a borrower's loan record and the financial reports he submits to SBA, specialists trained in financial counseling, financial statement analysis, and collection techniques are able to determine the small firm's progress.

The specialists maintain close contact with the firms whose loans they service. They visit indi- 17

New Programs of Interest to Small Business 

Pursuant to the revisions of the Trade Expansion Act of 1962, Public Law 87-794, and an amendment to the Small Business Act, Public 

Law 87-550 of July 25, 1962, the Small Business

Administration is empowered to make loans to assist any firm to adjust to changed economic conditions resulting from increased competition from imported articles, provided the Secretary of Commerce certifies the firm as eligible to apply for adjustment assistance. The financial assistance program is not yet operative, since funds to implement the activity have not been appropriated. 22

Section IV Aiding Victims of Disasters 

One of SBA's important and growing responsibilities is to make loans to assist victims of disasters. The agency aids small businessmen and others who have suffered property loss from storms or other natural disasters, and small businessmen who have suffered substantial economic injury. from drought or excessive rainfall, or from displacement by federally aided construction programs. 

Physical Damage Loans Are Broadest Activity 

The agency's oldest and most extensive disaster assistance program involves loans to persons whose property has been damaged or destroyed by floods, storms, or other natural disasters. Loans are made in areas designated disaster areas by the 

SBA Administrator.

Loans under this program are not limited to small businesses but are made to business concerns of any size, individuals, and nonprofit organizations such as churches and charitable institutions. 

There is no statutory limit on the amount of a loan. The amount is determined by the tangible net loss suffered, less any insurance benefits. The 

SBA charges 3 percent interest on a loan made entirely by the agency or on the agency's share of a loan made in participation with a bank. 

Economic Injury Loans Assist Small Business 

In its other disaster assistance programs, SBA makes loans to small firms that have suffered economic loss because of drought or excessive rainfall or displacement by federally aided construction programs. 

Drought and Excessive Rainfall Loans. These loans are made to small businesses in areas designated by the President or the Secretary of Agriculture. Loans may be of the participation or direct type and may be used for working capital, to replenish normal inventories, and for debt payment (except bank loans) which the borrower would have been able to meet had it not been for the loss of revenue due to the drought or excessive rainfall.

These loans, like physical damage loans, bear interest of 3 percent on SBA's share. 

Displaced Business Loans. Under an amendment to the Small Business Act included in the Housing Act of 1961, SBA makes loans to assist small firms forced to relocate because of federally aided urban renewal, highway, and other construction programs. The small firms must have suffered substantial economic injury as a result of the displacement.

The SBA's loans are in addition to compensation for moving expenses or direct loss of property paid by the local public agency conducting the renewal project, and are to help small firms relocate in suitable and reasonable locations. The amount that SBA may lend is based on a reasonable estimate of the cost of reestablishing the business. 

Loans of this type also may be made in participation with banks or directly by SBA. The interest rate on SBA's share is established annually on a statutory formula based on the average interest rate on all interest-bearing obligations of the United States, plus one-quarter of 1 percent. The present rate is 35% percent.

Agency Approves 

About 2,500 Loans

In 1963, SBA received 2,919 disaster loan applications and approved 2,503 for $30,136,000. (Approvals included some applications which had been pending at the start of the year.) 

Of the loans approved, 2,405 for $23,267,000 were physical damage loans; 7 for $156,000 were drought loans; 4 for $32,000 were excessive rainfall loans; and 87 for $6,681,000 were displaced business loans.

Through the end of 1963, SBA had approved a cumulative total of 21,413 disaster loans of all types for $205,816,000. 23

Section V Providing Long-Term Financing for Small Business Through SBIC's 

At the close of calendar year 1963, the 1,080th proposal to operate a small business investment company had been filed with the Small Business Administration, the result of 67 additional proposals being filed during this period. Although this reflects a decrease in the number of new proposals received for new SBIC's in comparison to the year 1962, this decline can be attributed in part to (1) the continuing effects of the 1962 stock market break, which had a major impact upon the program, and (2) the substantial number of companies that already have been licensed in certain areas. SBA now is putting more emphasis on the question of "need" in the area, and during the year contracted for a formal study of "saturation". 

This study is expected to develop criteria by which the saturation level of a given area might be measured.

During 1963, 65 licenses were issued to small business investment companies with a total capitalization of $22,260,724, including $10,305,500 in formation (SBA) funds. At the year's end, 16 licenses had been canceled, and the 692 SBIC's in operation were based in 42 States, the District of 

Columbia, Guam, Puerto Rico, and the Virgin

Islands. 

Funds Available for Financing

Small Firms Rise in 1963 

Funds available to SBIC's for investment in small concerns rose more than $40 million during calendar year 1963.

Million $ 8 22 18 48 42 It should be noted that although the number of proposals received and licenses issued decreased in number in comparison to the year 1962, new private subscriptions in licensed SBIC's amounted to $8 million during 1963. Most of these funds were subscribed by individuals who already were shareholders of SBIC's.

These shareholders thus evidenced their confidence in the future of the SBIC industry in the most concrete way-by investing more of their own money in it.

The SBIC's total capitalization of $595,747,804, as of the close of 1963, included net SBA commitments or disbursements in return for subordinated debentures of $117,873,683, or a ratio of 4 to 1 of private funds versus Government funds invested in the program.

During the year, 16 SBIC's surrendered their licenses, 4 as a result of mergers with other SBIC's, and 12 which decided not to continue in business. 

This raises the number of companies which have left the program to a cumulative total of 33, and these surrenders have taken almost $30 million out of the total capitalization of the program. It should be noted, however, that the SBIC's involved had already invested a considerable part of this amount in small business concerns prior to surrendering their licenses.

There continues to be three major reasons for the surrenders. One, the SBIC wanted to do things which SBA regulations would not permit, such as investing in a large business, buying listed securities, or acquiring a majority interest in small business; two, in activity as a result of disagreement in management about what kind of investments should be made; and three, developments in the directors' other business interests which precluded devoting the necessary time to the operation of the SBIC. 26

Operating Loan Activity Shows Increase 

734-160 0-64- -3 67 The Small Business Investment Act, as amended in 1961, provides that SBA may lend an SBIC up to $400,000 through purchase of subordinated debentures. These funds are made available for both formation and growth purposes, and on a matching-dollar basis with funds obtained by the SBIC through the issuance and sale of capital stock. 1962 1963 During 1963, SBA approved the purchase of $18.3 million of debentures of 108 licensed SBIC's to provide funds for growth purposes. At the end of the year, 16 additional applications for another $2.4 million were being processed.

The Small Business Investment Act also au thorizes the Small Business Administration to lend operating funds to an SBIC in an amount up to 50 percent of its paid-in capital and surplus (including any subordinated debentures held by SBA), with a maximum of $4 million of loans to any one company.

During 1963, lending activity under this section of the act continued to increase. By the end of 

1962, SBA had approved 218 operating loans for a total of $47.2 million. During 1963, 179 additional operating loans totaling almost $37 million were approved, raising the total outstanding to $84.2 million. Almost one-third of this $37 million approved during the past year was disbursed by banks under the agency's bank standby plan. This provides for the immediate assignment of the loan to the bank under the terms of a repurchase agreement, whereby the lending institution can deliver the note to SBA at any time and obtain the outstanding principal balance. 

As of December 31, 1963, 31 additional operating loan applications totaling $9.7 million were being processed.

Small business investment companies continued to expand their loan and investment activities in

At June 30, 1963, 620 SBIC's reported $421.3 million of financing outstanding through 8,471 transactions, amounting to 67.8 percent of their total assets.

The reports show that the distribution of financing by type changed significantly between June 

30, 1962, and June 30, 1963. As of the former date, gross loans and investments were distributed 35 percent to loans, 53 percent to debt securities, and 12 percent to capital stock of small business concerns. As of June 30, 1963, loans constituted 41 percent, debt securities 49 percent, and capital stock 10 percent.

The substantial increase in percentage of total assets devoted to loans and investments, from 51.3 percent at June 30, 1962, to 67.8 percent at June 30, 1963, marked an increasing maturity of the SBIC's as they found more opportunities for financing and less need to hold their funds in liquid assets. 

Along with this relative increase in the employment of funds for loans and investments came a parallel increase in total income in relation to total operating expenses. The combined statements of 

559 SBIC's showed, for the 3 months ended June 30, 1962, gross income of $6.7 million, or 171 percent of total operating expenses of $3.9 million. 

In the comparable period of 1963, the 620 SBIC's showed gross income of $9.4 million, which represented 220 percent of total operating expenses, of $4.3 million. During the first half of 1963, the most recent period for which figures are available, the trend in financing activity was toward nonmanufactur ing industries as contrasted with manufacturing industries. The percentage of total financing de voted to nonmanufacturing industries rose from 

57.6 to 59.5.

The distribution of financing among the nonmanufacturing activities obtaining the highest proportion of available funds remained relatively stable during the 6 months ended June 30, 1963. 

Of the total financing outstanding to both manufacturing and nonmanufacturing industries as of December 31, 1962, real estate enterprises had received 14 percent, retail trade 9 percent, building construction 6 percent, and wholesale trade 4 percent. As of June 30, 1963, corresponding figures were: real estate, 16 percent; retail trade, 9 percent; building construction, 7 percent; and wholesale trade, 4 percent.

Among the manufacturing industries, the larg est amounts of SBIC financing outstanding remained with manufacturers of professional, scientific, and other technical or specialized equipment, but the trend was toward financing of manufac turers of electrical machinery, equipment, and sup plies. The former had 10 percent of the total of all financing at December 31, 1962, and the latter 6 percent, whereas at June 30, 1963, the percentages had changed to 9 percent and 7 percent 

Regulatory Changes Increase SBIC's Responsibility for Their Operations 

SBA regulations governing small business in vestment companies were amended in 1963 princi pally to transfer additional responsibility to SBIC's as a result of their growing maturity. 

In one change, SBA eliminated the requirement that each SBIC file with SBA a verified copy of its fidelity bond. In another, the requirement for submission by licensed companies of a semiannual ac tivity report was withdrawn. This was mad possible by broadening the information supplied through the periodic financial reports to include such essential data from the activity report. 

In a few areas, it was necessary to impose addi 28

One amendment to the regulations restricted voluntary reductions of paid-in capital and paid-in surplus to not more than 10 percent in any one year, with certain exceptions, such permitted reductions to be made only by a pro rata distribution to shareholders. Another change now requires that any SBIC proposing to surrender its SBA license must first accomplish dissolution of the corporation on the basis of a plan previously approved by SBA.

Improving SBA Operations and Providing Additional Service 

During 1963 SBA installed an automatic writing machine controlled by paper-tape programming. It is used in conjunction with add-punch equipment already in service for preparation of financial and statistical summaries of SBIC data for publication. This improvement has accelerated the production of the summaries and has brought much greater accuracy to their final typing for reproduction. 200 150 100 50

Survey of Loan and Investment 

Losses Completed by SBA

In cooperation with the Treasury Department, and particularly the Internal Revenue Service, SBA circularized all SBIC's to obtain information as to their realized and estimated losses on loans and investments. The data supplied were used in deliberations concerning the need for legislation dealing with the impact of such losses on the financial stability of SBIC's and the methods of treating the losses in connection with taxation of income.

Forms and Instructions 

Revised by Agency

In 1963 the Instructions for Preparation of the 

Financial Report (SBA Form 468) were revised to provide for the furnishing by SBIC's of additional data some of which had previously been supplied through the Activity Report, SBA Form 

477, now discontinued. The Audit and Examina tion Guide and the Compliance Examination Re port, SBA Form 478, both used by independent public accountants in examining SBIC's, were brought up to date through revision.

Audits, Compliance Activities of SBIC's Expanded in 1963 

In 1961 the Investment Division initiated a program for the periodical examination of small business investment companies pursuant to the statutory authority conferred under section 308 (c) of the Small Business Investment Act. 

Under this program 158 companies were examined in 1963, as compared with 98 during the calendar year of 1962. Since the examination program was initiated, a total of 285 companies have been examined. An increase of 10 examiners to the staff of the Examinations Division within the past year has stepped up the number of examinations which can be conducted and it is anticipated that within the near future, SBA will be able to examine each licensee at least once every 

2 years.

The examinations have brought to light a variety of violations of the act and the regulations. 

Many of the violations uncovered have been of a minor nature and have been voluntarily corrected by the licensee. In a number of cases, the examination program uncovered more serious violations of the act which warranted further investigation and the institution of administrative or court proceedings.

Section 310 of the Small Business Investment 

Act authorizes the agency to make such investigations as necessary to determine whether a licensee or any other person has engaged or is about to engage in any acts or practices which constitute or will constitute a violation of any provision of the act, or of any rule or regulation under the act or any order issued under the act. The SBA is authorized, for this purpose, to administer oaths, subpena witnesses, compel their attendance, take evidence and require the production of records.

A number of investigations were instituted by SBA in 1963, some of which already have led to the institution of proceedings by the Department of Justice, and a suit by SBA for an injunction and the appointment of a receiver. A number of investigations begun in 1963 are continuing and may result in additional judicial proceedings. The SBA is given the power by section 309 of the act to suspend a license (1) for false statements knowingly made for the purpose of obtaining the license; (2) if any written statement for the purpose of obtaining a license fails to state a material fact; (3) for willful or repeated violation of, or willful or repeated failure to observe any provision of this act or of any rule or regulation authorized by the act; or (4) for violation of or failure to observe any cease-and-desist order issued by the SBA.

No licenses were suspended during 1963 by the agency. However, preliminary steps were initiated for the suspension of licenses and a number of these proceedings probably will be started in 1964 as a result of investigations. 

In at least one instance in 1963 the voluntary surrender of a license precluded the necessity of instituting administrative action for the suspension or revocation of the license. 31

Section VI Assisting With Community Development 

SBA has several programs which specifically are designed to raise the economic level of States and communities. These programs help States and communities expand their established industries or add new ones, and in this way increase markets for their products, and jobs and income for their citizens. (1) A reorganization aimed at the fuller and more effective utilization of staff. Heretofore, the primary responsibility for implementing the local development company loan program rested with personnel of the Investment Division. While the Investment Division still has the responsibility for implementing the program, personnel of the Financial Assistance Division in the field offices also are being utilized. (2) Fuller and more effective utilization of field staff has been accomplished by (a) extending case-processing responsibility to the branch office level, and (b) delegating action authority (to approve or decline) to the regional offices for direct loans up to $50,000 and bank participation loans up to $100,000. This is accomplishing results as is evidenced by an increased number of loans within these loan levels.

These programs include SBA loans to State and local development companies for use in financing small businesses, and SBA cooperation in local- State-Federal Government programs to redevelop areas of chronic and substantial unemployment and underemployment.

Development Loan Activity Continues Rise in 1963 Under the Small Business Investment Act, SBA 

makes loans to local development companies for use in helping specific small businesses to expand or modernize their facilities, or in helping new small firms get started. The agency also lends to eligible State development companies for the general purpose of providing long-term loans and equity financing to small firms, providing funds are not available on reasonable terms to the small businesses from other sources.

Demand for local development company loans increased in 1963, continuing an uptrend which began after Congress amended the Small Business Investment Act in 1961 to liberalize loans to assist small businesses by raising the maximum loan amount from $250,000 to $350,000. Interest also was heightened by SBA's increased help in areas designated as suffering from substantial unemployment and low income where interest rates on these loans were lowered to 4 percent and priorities were assigned to small firms in those areas. 

During 1963 the increased number of communities participating in the local development company loan program also was attributable to a fourpronged revision of the program's operation. The four changes instituted in the program are: (3) New emphasis and effort to develop cooperative programs with other Federal agencies having related economic development programs. 

By means of such cooperative effort, the local development company loan program will be made immediately available to those communities which as a result of the economic programs of other agencies, gain new opportunities for the establishment and expansion of their small businesses. 

(4) Development of cooperative programs where possible with public and private, State and local organizations engaged in economic development.

Local Development Company Loans. SBA 

approved 115 loans for $18.2 million to help local development companies assist small businesses in 1963. These projects provided an estimated 6,200 new job opportunities. In the prior year SBA approved 100 loans for $14.1 million.

The above figures for calendar 1963 compare with cumulative figures as of December 31, 1963, which show 375 loans for $51.9 million approved since the start of the program in August 1958. 32

The small business projects financed through local levelopment company loans have provided employment for about 21,400 people as of the close of 963, according to information filed with SBA. 

This figure represents direct employment only and does not include the indirect jobs created through onstruction and other service-related activities. 

The growing local development company loan program is especially applicable in the smaller, ural communities, many of which are suffering from increased unemployment because of decreasng job opportunities. SBA development company loans have made it possible for them to diversify their economy and thus create new job 

Opportunities. 

State Development Company Loans. Cumula tively through the close of 1963, SBA had approved 13 loans to State development companies for a total of $9,233,750. During 1963 the agency received one application from a State development company for $500,000. Most of the proceeds of these loans are used for plant construction, con- 500 100 080 200 100

SITE OF GRAFTON ALL AMERICA CITY PLANT 

Grafton, West Virginia, once a prosperous industrial coal producing and railroad town suffered severe economic setbacks with the closing of several plants due to merger and the decline of mining and railroad operations. Recognizing the need for community action, its leaders formed a local development company. With the aid of SBA loans, it was able to finance new industry and help area firms to expand. 

In 1963, Grafton was one of the 10 American cities to win the LOOK Magazine award as "An All American City."

This Federal assistance and community participation made possible the growth of a small business, provided more jobs for local townspeople and increased the production of modern equipment to improve farming.

During the year SBA continued to work closely with ARA, with other Federal agencies, and with State and local authorities in a joint effort to establish and expand industry and commerce in areas designated by ARA for redevelopment. 

Private enterprise, local initiative, and public investment are the foundation of ARA's commercial and industrial loans processed by SBA. 

These loans, now processed in an average of less than 60 days, are used as "seed money" to augment local private and public investment in business expansion or for new projects. Such loans, however, are available only to the extent that the communities and business firms involved cannot finance the total cost on reasonable terms through other channels.

During 1963 considerable emphasis was placed on staffing and training of an effective field force of specialists which are organized as teams to assist communities in planning and financing ARA redevelopment projects. These teams specialize in financial counseling, advisory engineering and legal services, market analysis, appraisals, new product development and economic feasibility studies. The teams are staffed at local levels, and work closely with ARA to develop and finance projects for permanent community progress. 

In cases where a small business project needs money for purposes for which ARA funds cannot be used (working capital, for example), and funds are unavailable from a State or local source or from another Federal agency, SBA may provide the needed financing through its business loan program. SBA also may help to finance small business projects under its development company loan program.

During 1963, SBA made 20 working capital loans to ARA applicants in the amount of $2,906,050. 36

Section VII Helping Small Firms Get Government Contracts have increased in value from $161,000 in calendar year 1954 to $1.9 billion last year.

A major function and goal of the Small Business Administration is to help small firms obtain a larger share of the Federal Government's contracts, both as prime contractors and subcontractors.

Additional data on the set-aside program are presented later in this section under "Small Business Share of Government Purchasing."

Highlights of the assistance given small firms in 1963 were: 

1. The SBA and Government purchasing agencies jointly set aside a record $2.4 billion of purchases to be made exclusively from small business. 

The set-asides resulted in award of $1.9 billion of contracts to small firms. 2. The SBA obtained greater opportunity for small firms to compete for specific contracts, supplied the names of many small suppliers on request of contracting agencies, and helped these agencies open up "sole-source" purchases to competitive bidding by small business. 

3. The SBA worked with major prime contractors and Government buying agencies to increase subcontracting awards to small firms. 

Progress was evidenced by an increase in subcontracting payments to small firms by large defense contractors and by agreement of 27 major prime contractors to seek SBA help in locating qualified small subcontractors.

Set-Asides for Small Firms at Record Level in 1963

Last year, SBA and Government purchasing agencies jointly set aside 45,628 proposed purchases valued at $2.4 billion for competitive bidding by small firms. These set-asides resulted in award to small firms of 48,664 contracts valued at $1.9 billion. In 1962, SBA and purchasing agencies jointly reserved for small business 44,174 proposed purchases valued at $2.2 billion; they resulted in 54,179 contracts totaling $1.8 billion.

The SBA's joint set-aside program with purchasing agencies began on August 1, 1953. Since then, it has resulted in awards to small firms of 

254,258 contracts valued at $9.3 billion. Awards

How the Program Helps Small Firms. Dur ing one 2-month period last year, $32.5 million of

Government contracts for jet fuel were awarded to small refineries because the proposed purchases were earmarked for small business by the Defense Department and SBA. Subsequently, the two agencies jointly set aside another $59.1 million of contracts for bidding by small firms. 

In another typical case, SBA and Navy officials reserved 14 purchases of aircraft parts, formerly obtained on a "sole-source" basis from one supplier, for small business bidding. The competition among small firms resulted in lower prices. The 

14 contract awards totaled about $300,000; at previous prices, the same items would have cost an additional $336,000.

The agency's cooperative program with the Department of Defense resulted in 33,239 small business set-asides in fiscal 1963, covering purchases totaling $1.9 billion; contract awards under these set-asides totaled 35,388, for $1.5 billion.

The cooperative program with National Aero nautics and Space Administration, General Serv ices Administration, and other civilian agencies resulted in 9,647 set-asides in fiscal 1963, covering contracts totaling $410 million; contract awards under these set-asides totaled 10,968, for $346 million.

Agency Finds Additional Small Business Bidders 

SBA representatives at major buying centers continually review proposed purchases for setaside and related purposes. Whenever they find purchases that small firms can supply but that few or none have bid on in the past, they seek addi- 37

tional bidders from SBA's Facilities Inventory or other sources.

By increasing small business competition in this way, the agency helps obtain a better price on Government purchases. It also helps to broaden the base of Government suppliers and strengthen the small business economy.

Here are two recent cases which illustrate the value of SBA's efforts:

In one case, SBA asked that a small research and development firm in California be allowed to submit a proposal on a contract for a solar magnetic aspect sensor. Permission was given, and the small firm bid on and won the contract. The original contract, for $89,000, has since been increased to $150,000.

In another instance, SBA arranged for a small manufacturer to get a bid set from the Federal Aviation Agency on a purchase that specified the product of a large business or an equal product. 

The small firm was the low bidder, and was awarded a contract for $160,000. This was more than $35,000 less than the bid by the large business. 

Agency Advises, Assists With Prime Subcontracts 

The SBA advises and assists small firms in obtaining Government prime contracts and subcontracts, helping them to learn which agencies or prime contractors buy the products or services they offer and guiding them in developing opportunities to bid. The SBA also helps small firms resolve problems arising out of contracts with Government agencies and prime contractors.

A typical example of such assistance involved a purchase of test tubes by the Defense Medical Supply Center. The low bidder, a large firm, had taken exception to the pertinent specifications. 

The SBA advised the second lowest bidder, a small firm, concerning its right to protest the award being made to the low bidder under these circumstances. As a result, the small firm protested and the large firm's bid was declared nonresponsive. 

The small firm was awarded the contract, valued at approximately $103,000.

Increased Subcontract Awards to Small Business Concerns The Government's Small Business Subcontract ing Program is administered jointly by SBA, the 

Defense Department, and the General Services Administration. Regulations implementing the program were issued in February 1962, and are a part of the Armed Services Procurement Regulations (ASPR) and the Federal Procurement 

Regulations (FPR). Major provisions instruct military and civilian contracting personnel to include in contracts over $500,000 clauses requiring prime contractors to establish small business subcontracting programs. In addition, where contracts are over $5,000 estimated cost, contractors are "urged to accomplish the maximum amount of subcontracting which they consider to be consistent with the efficient performance of the contract." 

A report of the Defense Department for fiscal year 1963 shows an increase in payments to small firms by the large defense contractors reporting un der the Defense Subcontracting Program. Dur ing fiscal 1963, small firms received $4,341,258,000 in subcontracting payments, compared with $4,010,720,000 during fiscal 1962. 

The 1963 gain in small business subcontracts was achieved despite the increasingly stringent requirements of the space age exploration program and the resulting technological difficulties for small firms.

Supplementing the Government-wide small business subcontracting program, SBA is working closely with the top 27 defense contractors. By providing them the names of qualified small suppliers, SBA seeks to increase small business competition for their purchases. Except where contracts are of such size that the contracting agency has specifically retained the right of contract approval, this program is based entirely on the willingness of prime contractors to solicit SBA aid. 

Since the program was initiated, SBA's subcontracting staff has met with officials of nearly 200 major purchasing offices of the 27 prime contractors. 

Prime contractors are now actively seeking SBA assistance in locating capable small firms. Indications are that this prime contractor-SBA cooperation will encourage other prime contractors to greater efforts in behalf of small business. 

SBA Certifies to Small Firms' Ability To Perform Contracts The SBA's Certificate of Competency (COC) 

Program provides, in effect, an appeals procedure for the small concern that bids low on a contract, but whose bid is rejected for reasons of inadequate productive capacity or credit. If the small firm appeals to SBA, the agency makes an independent study of its capabilities. If SBA certifies that the small firm can carry out the 38

A small manufacturer in Massachusetts was denied a contract by a Defense Agency to produce 12,450 insulated steel water canteens because of lack of production capacity. 

SBA investigated and found the company competent to execute the order. A contract award, amounting to $99,326, was issued by the procuring agency. 

A Missouri mill, with an annual payroll of $250,000 and a Certificate of Competency from SBA, was awarded a $129,669 contract to produce 18,568 blankets for a Defense 

Supply Agency. As a result of this Federal contract, 12 more employees were hired. The order was completed two weeks ahead of schedule.

SBA HELPS SMALL BUSINESS FIRMS GET U. S. GOVERNMENT CONTRACTS Through Certificates of Competency and Set-Asides SBA Assists Small Business and Saves the Government Money An SBA Certificate of Competency boosted a small Florida aircraft repair company's payroll from 100 to 500 employees. The U. S. Air Force issued the firm a contract to repair small jet aircraft. SBA is credited by the company. with significant help in obtaining this business. 

A small Nebraska construction company proved to the U. S. 

Corps of Engineers its ability to perform an emergency repair job on the Missouri River with the aid of an SBA Certificate of Competency. The work was completed ahead of schedule, and at a saving to the Government.

contract, the certification is binding on the contracting agency.

This SBA service has enabled many small companies to obtain Government contracts which otherwise would have gone to higher bidders. 

During 1963, SBA issued 155 COC's relating to contracts valued at $33,377,000. (Contract awards in 1963 resulting from COC's are listed in app. A.)

The 1,437 Certificates of Competency issued since 1953 have resulted in 1,316 contracts for approximately $258 million. 

The case of one small company in the Appalachian Region illustrates the effectiveness of this program. 

In May 1962, SBA certified to this small firm's ability to carry out a $1 million contract for mine detectors. The company was awarded the contract and is performing satisfactorily. In June 

1962, the agency issued a second COC to the company, covering a $1.5 million contract for periscopes. The contract was completed 3 months ahead of schedule. In September 1962, SBA certified to the company's ability to undertake a $1.7 million contract for an infrared weapons sight. 

The preproduction samples made in accordance with Government drawings and specifications proved unsatisfactory. The company then asked and was given permission to redesign a part of the item. The procuring agency stated that the company's redesign resulted in the best weapon of its type the Army has ever had. 

In 1963 the company was awarded additional Army contracts without COC action by SBA. At the time the company was first referred to SBA, it employed about 20 people. The number has increased to more than 250 and will probably go to more than 325 at peak production on the military contracts awarded in 1963. This represents a substantial increase in jobs in an area plagued by heavy unemployment. 

"Sole Source" Purchases Opened to Small Firms 

The SBA and purchasing agencies helped develop small business competition on many purchases which previously had been made on a "sole source" basis. On many of these purchases, the 

Government was able to obtain a lower price as a result of competition where none had existed on prior procurements.

Typical of the joint "breakout" effort is the case of a Navy purchase of 8,118 electronic switches which were previously purchased on a sole-source basis from large business. Small suppliers were found by SBA and the procurement was set-aside 100 percent for small business. The contract was awarded to a small firm at a unit price of $2.37 compared to the previous price of $10.36. This reflected a total price reduction of $64,862.82 on one procurement. By introducing new competition, six qualified small firms were established as suppliers of this item. 

On another procurement, a small firm was awarded a contract for generator repair kits valued at $100,940 as a result of cooperation by an 

SBA representative and Air Force procurement officials. The item was formerly a sole-source purchase from large business. The specifications and drawings were not adequate for a competitive purchase. The SBA representative convinced procurement officials that a known small manufacturer could make the kit with the limited data available. 

As a result, the small firm was allowed to bid. 

The bid price of the only other bidder, the previous sole source manufacturer, was $131,741. The 

Government thus awarded this contract at a price $30,800 less than could have been obtained from the large supplier.

Small Firms May Pool Facilities 

The SBA approves the association of groups of small firms for purposes of defense production, research and development, and acquisition of raw materials and equipment. Such groups are then known as "small business pools" and are exempt from the provisions of antitrust laws and the Fed eral Trade Commission Act. 

At the close of 1963, 10 pools were in operation, comprised of 74 small firms with a total of 3,917 employees. 

Four pools received 16 Government prime contracts or subcontracts in 1963 valued at approximately $564,000. The existing pools have been awarded over 500 contracts totaling approximately $2.5 million.

The SBA also participates with other Government agencies, prime contractors, manufacturers, trade associations, and educational groups in broader conferences designed to help businessmen strengthen their domestic operations or develop and expand markets abroad.
