SBA Helps Firms With Specifications Problems 

Small business bidders on Government procurements often have difficulty in obtaining all of the applicable specifications in time to bid properly on an item. The SBA publication "U.S. Government Purchasing Specifications and Sales Directory" helps such companies to locate specifications and indexes by listing locations of reference libraries and providing other information. Proposed new specifications and changes to existing specifications are reviewed by SBA technical staff to determine whether they are proprietary or discriminatory against small business. In such cases 

SBA works with the responsible agencies to remove the restrictive provisions. 

Recently a small business concern requested SBA assistance in removing a provision that had been written into a specification for runway and highway marking paint that permitted the acceptance of a lower grade material in lieu of the original higher quality and more costly material. 

The company also claimed that the specifications were proprietary to one company's product, in effect, by reference made to a second proprietary specification. The two specifications were analyzed and discussed with the agencies. The specifications are now being changed and combined to provide, to the maximum extent possible, for reasonable competition and, at the same time, assure the Government of the intended quality product. 

Agency Inventory Lists Productive Facilities 

The SBA's facilities inventory is an essential tool for carrying out national SBA programs to foster and promote the general economic health of small business. The inventory includes five types of productive facilities: industrial production, research and development, forest products, construction contractor, and contractor services.

At the end of 1963 there were a total of 53,541 small firms registered in the inventory. Plans are currently underway to transfer the data from punchcards to magnetic tapes to better serve SBA and other Federal agencies.

The facilities inventory is used by the SBA field offices in support of SBA's contracts assistance and other programs. It is used also by other Federal agencies, and State and municipal governments in programs of economic development. 

Moreover, the Industrial Production Facilities 

Register is the principal source of information on the location and capabilities of small manufacturing companies in the United States for national defense planning.

The facilities inventory is the principal source used by SBA to identify small firms interested in and capable of bidding on suitable Government contracts or subcontracts. Also, it is used to notify small firms registered with SBA of impending Government contracts and subcontracts. 

The use of the facilities inventory by other Government agencies increased substantially during calendar year 1963. Typical was the Veterans' 

Administration which, in March 1963, asked SBA to cooperate in a survey they were making of procurement practices at Veterans' Administration installations and the availability of suitable local sources of supply for a wide range of products and services. Each SBA region was requested to send to Washington a photocopy of registration forms for small firms in certain categories of the construction contractor and contractor services registers. Registration forms for 

3,500 companies were submitted. These data were used by the Veteran's Administration to evaluate their programs principally involving local purchase-service-type contracts at various VA field installations.

In 1963, the Small Business Administration continued to work closely with the Departments of the Army, Navy, Air Force, and the

These agencies provide advance information to SBA regarding specific future research and development contracts so that the agency can locate additional sources capable of performing these contracts. During 1963, SBA furnished the names of over 3,000 small firms that were capable and available to perform contract research and development. SBA Helps Small Firms Buy From the Government 

48, 938 75, 801 69, 533 3, 698 

158, 266 356, 236 An important SBA responsibility is to help small firms purchase a fair share of property being sold by the Government. Under SBA agreements with other Federal agencies, Government sales frequently are earmarked for exclusive offering to small firms. These include sales of Federal surplus personal property, strategic materials from the national stockpile, and timber from Federal forests. As another part of this SBA service, the agency notifies small concerns of forthcoming Government sales of machine tools, trucks, construction equipment, and other property. 

During 1963, SBA and the General Services Administration jointly set aside for small business a sale of 6,300 tons of pig aluminum under the disposal program authorized by Public Law 88-8. 

In another instance under the same cooperative program three sales of cadmium, amounting to 569,000 pounds, were set aside for exclusive bidding by small firms.

In the field of forest products, SBA has small business set-aside agreements with the Forest Service of the Department of Agriculture, the

In 1963, SBA and cooperating agencies jointly set aside 123 timber sales amounting to 404,733,000 board feet, with an estimated value of $4.3 million. 

The SBA helps small firms locate property being sold by the Federal Government for which the firms have a specific need. By the close of 

1963, 14,940 small firms had registered their needs with SBA.

During the year, 1,836 registered small firms purchased 5,303 Government items for $3,294,669.

This property cost them an average of 20 to 30 percent of the original price, which represented a saving to the buyer and a good return to the

Section VIII Strengthening Small Business Management Continued expansion of the Nation's economy in 

1963 brought new opportunities and new challenges to small business. Changes in urban and rural markets, development of new products in response to higher incomes, new methods of production and distribution-these and other changes imposed new demands on business management. ing entering business. The workshops are held in 

SBA field offices, with agency specialists discussing the financial, managerial, and legal aspects of starting a business. Subjects include choice of a location and form of organization, management requirements, taxes, regulations, and financial needs. 

To help small operators adjust to and profit from change, SBA strengthened and broadened its programs of management assistance to small business in these ways: 

1. Began a program of 1-day workshops to inform prospective small business owners of factors they should consider before starting their own enterprises. 

2. Expanded the Intra-Industry Management 

Program (IMP), which is a cooperative effort by large firms, trade associations, and SBA to improve small business management. 

3. Cosponsored with educational institutions and others a record number of management courses for small businessmen. 

4. Cosponsored with other agencies numerous small business conferences on important subjects such as tax management and export trade. 

5. Stepped up the publication of helpful management information, and the assistance given individual small firms with new product and other problems.

SBA Workshops Aid and Guide Prospective Small Businessmen 

During 1963, SBA began a new management service-workshops for those who are thinking of establishing their own small firms. 

Many new businesses fail after the first few years because the owners commit their talent and capital with only a partial understanding and knowledge of the total requirements. To help prevent such errors, SBA last year began conducting 1-day workshops for persons who are consider Fifty-two workshops were conducted last year, the first 11 on an experimental basis. The response was so enthusiastic the workshops have been made a permanent program, with 135 scheduled to be given by mid-1964.

These comments by workshop participants indicate the results:

A prospective business owner in New York City wrote her Congressman that a workshop she attended was "the best organized, most informative and most enthusiastic seminar I ever attended ... 

I appreciate the Government's making it possible to draw upon the experience of the experts at SBA." 

An Illinois businessman who was considering buying a second small business said that "the session was most helpful in clearing up puzzling questions.' 

Another prospective business owner said: "You certainly must derive a great deal of satisfaction from the assistance that the 'Workshop for 

Prospective Small Business Owners' offers. In my opinion, you provide a very necessary service for small business and the country's economy as well." 99 SBA Expands 

Scope of IMP In the Intra-Industry Management Program, SBA works with large firms and trade associations to improve the management skills of their small suppliers, dealers, distributors, and members. The primary goal is to enable these small businessmen to earn larger profits for their own, their communities', and the Nation's benefit. .47

However, the program also provides other benefits for both sponsor and recipient. These include decreased dealer and supplier turnover, more effective marketing of the producer's products, and stronger reciprocal loyalties. 

The SBA's role in IMP is limited to helping large firms and associations develop management improvement programs for small customers and members and to providing useful management ideas. Thus the program is of nominal cost to the taxpayers.

During 1963, SBA gave much effort to enlisting participation of additional large firms and trade associations in IMP. It also staffed additional field offices with IMP specialists to handle the 

New participants during the year included a large finance corporation which established a program to help automobile dealers improve their management, and a large manufacturing concern which began using SBA publications to upgrade its dealers' management skills.

The executive secretary of a west coast trade association which sponsored a program for its members wrote: "Thank you for your assistance in arranging our first Workshop on Business Practices. We hope, with your continued interest, to develop other programs to help our members further improve their skills."

By the end of 1963, IMP already had stimulated and assisted in the development of management assistance programs carried on by large organizations covering some 150,000 small businesses and was being geared to reach over 200,000.

Management Courses Aid More Business Owners 

The administrative management course program cosponsored by SBA and educational institutions made record progress last year, both in number of courses and number of businessmen-enrollees. A total of 19,631 small business owners and managers. attended 627 courses. Enrollment increased by more than 4,000 over 1962, thanks largely to efforts of the 278 cosponsoring educational institutions. 

Since the beginning of the course program in 1954, a cumulative 700 universities, colleges, local adult education programs, and business and civic organizations have cosponsored 2,427 courses with total attendance exceeding 76,000. The record 

1963 activity compared with 1962-a previous record year-is:

Business Owners Profit. Tangible benefits re ceived from the courses are evidenced by these 

A building contractor pointed out, "I'm sure we could reduce the number of failures if only everyone could attend the course on good bookkeeping and recordkeeping."

Another small business owner called a course he attended "the most valuable 15 hours ever spent for our company."

A third small business owner said, "I wish I had had this chance 20 years ago.' 99 Continuing Education. Many small businessmen tell SBA they need continuing and more extensive management education. To meet their need, SBA cosponsors advanced courses which concentrate on one or a few aspects of management. In 1963 advanced courses made up 41 per- cent of the year's total. The percentage has increased each year as more small business owners and managers elect to study in depth one or more subjects previously taken.

Decisionmaking Simulations. Ten courses in 

1963 utilized the technique of business simulation exercises, or "business games." All featured "Mantrap," an exercise prepared especially for small business owners. These exercises were first tested on a few groups, then rewritten to include the experience the trial runs developed. Reception was excellent and more courses will use the games in 1964.

Packaged Course Material. Work progressed on packaged materials for course leaders, including a course outline, subject materials, visual materials, suggested readings, and bibliographies. 

This program developed from SBA discussions with the Distributive Education Branch of the Department of Health, Education, and Welfare, and the American Vocational Association. Pre liminary research was completed on 17 subjects in 1963, with plans for release of a major portion of the packages of subject materials in 1964. 

Television Tapes and Films. Television tapes and sound films continued to be used to advantage in SBA's management courses. A 16-mm. sound film of an eight-session administrative manage- 48

Altogether the tapes and films have been shown in more than 20 States, with wide diffusion of the materials. For example, four educational stations in one Southern State enlarged their audiences by dividing a 1-hour program into 30-minute sessions and scheduling them at times convenient to businessmen.

Management Conferences Reach Record High 

The SBA cosponsored 161 management conferences last year, compared with a previous high of 132 in 1962. Attendance of 19,000 exceeded the 1962 total by 21 percent. Two types of conferences which accounted for nearly one-half the total attendance were: 

Tax Management Conferences. The SBA and the cosponsoring Internal Revenue Service held 

55 tax conferences attended by 6,000 businessmen, compared with 22 conferences and 4,500 attendance in 1962. The conferences informed small businessmen of their obligations and rights under the tax laws.

Export Trade Management Conferences. The 

SBA and the cosponsoring Department of Commerce conducted 35 conferences on export trade, and nearly 3,000 small businessmen attended. The sessions were designed to stimulate interest in exports as a means of enlarging production and sales.

Management Publications 

Go to Wide Audience

One of SBA's most effective means of providing management information to small business is through several series of helpful publications. 

Thirty-two new leaflets and booklets on important management subjects were issued by the agency last year. Distribution during 1963 of all publications totaled nearly 3 million copies and was made on either a for-sale or individualrequest basis. In addition to its regular series of management publications, SBA issued 98 summaries of studies it financed through research grants. Distribution of these summaries, which is discussed later, amounted to more than 3 million copies. 

Secondary Readership. In addition to primary distribution, a secondary readership of SBA publications develops through reprints in trade association bulletins, trade and professional journals, and customer-relations literature issued by private firms. Readers who pass the publications on to employees, colleagues, clients, or customers further enlarge circulation.

In general, readership encompasses four groups: (1) small retailers, wholesalers, servicetrade operators, and manufacturers; (2) prospective small business owners and managers; (3) those who deal with and advise small businessmen, such as accountants, bankers, and consultants; and 

Subject Matter. Subjects covered in 1963 

widened the spectrum of SBA management information supplied to businessmen. For example, the Management Research Summary series referred to earlier included studies of area development, communications, business opportunities, business organization, financing and financial analysis, management counseling, factors contributing to success or failure, taxes, and foreign trade. 

Noteworthy among subjects covered in the regular series were financial management and business-government relations. 

Prospective Small Businesses. In its publications as in its workshops, SBA gave special attention last year to the needs of prospective owner-managers. New publications issued included booklets on "Starting and Managing an

Aviation Fixed-Base Operation," "Starting and 

Managing a Small Motel," and "Starting and

Managing a Small Duplicating and Mailing Serv ice." SBA booklets of this type discuss each aspect of starting a business, including the rewards and the pitfalls.

Fourteen of the Management Research Sum maries issued in 1963 also provided information on starting a new firm. 

Reader Response. Small firm owners and managers have continued to make good use of SBA management publications. 

From a small research and development firm came this about a Technical Aid: "It helped us replan our electric power needs. . . provided excellent checklists and, most important, helped us perform more efficiently."

An electric appliance dealer said that a Small Marketers Aid led him to revise his cost accounting for special orders "at an initial $500 saving." 51

Also commenting on this Aid, a small office supplier said: "I give the credit for increasing my service business solely to it." 

A hardware dealer who had bought a controlling interest in a manufacturing plant wrote: "I can truthfully say that the background that shaped my decision came from reading your publications." 

The founder of a family-owned summertime roadstand told SBA: "Your articles have provided us merchandising techniques that increased our gross sales, as well as cost-saving ideas making the roadstand a year-round business." 

SBA-Financed Research Helps Small Business 

As another part of its broad program of management assistance, SBA finances research into problems which are peculiar to small firms. 

Agency-sponsored research is done under grants made in fiscal years 1959 and 1960, and may be conducted under contract. 

Grant Research. Publication of 98 manage ment research reports last year underscored SBA's intent to make the results of grant research promptly available. By the yearend, a cumulative total of 176 studies had resulted from 105 research grants. When all studies have been completed, the findings will be embodied in 235 reports. These reports are distributed on a for-sale basis by institutions that received grants.

The research work has included national as well as State and locally oriented research projects. 

For example, the University of Texas in Austin and the University of Houston researched and developed decisionmaking games of national small business significance. Studies by other grantees have dealt with State and community interests and needs, such as a University of Alaska study of ways to stimulate the tourist industry in that 

State. 

The year was marked by expanding use of the agency's management research material by business and trade associations; lawyers, bankers, accountants; small business counselors; educators in adult training courses, and legislative personnel. 

A State legislative committee, for example, reproduced and distributed one report to more than 500 small firms. Congressional legislative committees and Congressmen used grant studies as background material when considering and preparing legislation pertaining to small business. A trade association reproduced and sent several complete reports to its 2,500 members. Orders for more than 2,500 copies of the report "Buying and

Selling a Small Business" were received by its producer, the University of Wichita, within a few weeks after publication. 

For a list of grant reports approved by SBA in 1963, see appendix B.

SBA Counseling Activity Increases 10 Percent 

Through personal counseling, SBA management specialists provide small business owners or prospective owners with answers to questions on specific problems.

In 1963, Washington and field office counselors assisted with about 18,000 problems, 10 percent more than in 1962 and 35 percent more than in 1961. 

The problems directed to SBA ranged from one individual's asking how to promote the sale of sunglasses to another's asking how to cope with multiple restaurant taxes. One prospective business owner asked for sources of financing for a women's ready-to-wear store; another, how to establish a home cleaning service.

In an updating of counseling material, 22 Small Business Bulletins were completely revised and 5 new bibliographies compiled, of which 2, "Machine Shop-Job Type" and "Automatic Merchandising," were scheduled for release in early 1964.

Also revised were 33 titles of another management tool, "Counseling Notes," which are brief outlines of the basic requirements for starting a particular type of small business. 

New Product Development Essential to Small Firms 

Development of new products is essential to small business growth in a competitive economy. 

Moreover, for the individual small firm, new product development often proves the difference between success and failure. During 1963, SBA realined its products assistance programs to provide more effective aid to small firms. 

About 3,000 small firms were assisted during the year. Problems on which SBA helped covered packaging, distribution, use of new materials, and new processes. Another 1,000 firms were guided on use of research and development laboratories and consultants; other hundreds were referred to technical and management reports. 

Many small firms were given guidance on Gov- 52

ernment patents as a source of new products. The agency took part in 18 invention and new product shows and these resulted in counseling of nearly 

800 businessmen.

To improve its Products List Circular which describes Government and privately owned patents available for license or sale to small businessmen, the agency converted this formerly free publication into a monthly for-sale periodical, available at $1.50 per year from the Superintendent of Documents. There were more than 7,300 subscribers at the yearend, and additional format improvements were being made to increase usefulness and sales.

Briefing Foreign Visitors on SBA's Management Programs 

Foreign government officials and businessmen from five continents were briefed on SBA management assistance programs during visits to the agency's Washington and field offices. Whether from Japan or Brazil, Nyasaland or the Philippines, France or Pakistan, these visitors were eager to learn how our Nation fosters and encourages small business. Many indicated they would urge their own governments, trade associations, and business groups to adopt similar programs. 53

Section IX Equality for Minority Small Businessmen The SBA Administrator last year investigated the extent to which SBA's services were being made equally available to all small businessmen. 

He found that among certain groups of minority small businessmen, principally Negroes, the services were little known and little used.

For example, in a metropolitan area which has thousands of Negro small businessmen, and which is the location of an SBA regional office, loans to these businessmen averaged considerably less than one per year since the start of the agency. In another area where there are many hundreds of small firms operated by Negroes, and which is served by an SBA branch office, the lending rate was only a little higher. 

To remedy this condition, the Administrator, on 

September 3, 1963, took these actions: • Set forth a policy that no small businessman, otherwise eligible, should be denied equal opportunity to receive any SBA service because of race, creed, color, or national origin. • Established an office charged with informing minority groups about the availability of SBA's services. 

Specific responsibilities of this new office are: 

1. Determining, by independent investigation and review, field compliance with the SBA policy of equal accessibility to agency services. 

2. Maintaining liaison between SBA and representatives of minority groups through whom the nature and availability of the programs and the equality policy can be explained to minority small businessmen ; 

3. Keeping the Administrator advised whether programs are being maintained on a basis of equal accessibility; 

4. In collaboration with Deputy Administrators, Regional Directors, and other SBA officials, taking any needed corrective actions and, where complex situations require action by the Administrator, reporting to him.

Appointed head of the office with the title of 

Special Assistant to the Administrator was Randall L. Tyus, then associate director of development for Clark College, Atlanta, Ga., and previously an investment firm counselor, a national representative of a chemical manufacturer, and consultant to a food processing firm. Implementing the 

Equality Policy 

As the first step in implementing the equality policy, the Special Assistant's office, immediately after its establishment, opened channels through which the policy and the SBA services could be made better known to minority small businessmen.

Of special help in this effort was the School of 

Journalism of Lincoln University, Jefferson City, Mo., which provided SBA the names of 142 Negro weeklies to which distribution of all news releases on SBA services was quickly begun.

The Special Assistant discussed fully the SBA 

policy and services before various Negro business groups, including the Washington Business 

League, Washington, D.C.; Conference of the National Business League, Atlanta, Ga.; and the 

Frontier Club, Atlanta; he appeared also on a special program conducted by TV station WOOK, Washington, D.C., and was interviewed on a program carried from Washington over 25 radio stations. 

Also informed of the policy and services, with special reference to possible use and cosponsorship of SBA educational programs, were such Negro institutions of higher learning as Fisk University, Nashville, Tenn.; Dillard University, New Orleans, La.; Wiley College, Marshall, Tex.; Saint 

Augustine's College, Raleigh, N.C.; and Howard 

University, Washington, D.C. 54

Response to the

Information Effort 

That the SBA services were needed by Negro small businessmen became evident from the volume of requests for further information and assistance that flowed by mail, telephone, and personal visits into SBA field offices and the 

Washington office.

Mail and telephone inquiries came to the Washington office from every part of the Nation. The greatest number came from the Middle West and the Atlantic seaboard, but there was a substantial volume also from New England, the South, the Far West, and the Pacific coast. 

In some instances, Negro small businessmen journeyed personally to Washington from distant points to discuss their eligibility for business loans. "War on Poverty"; a Pilot Program

Could SBA loans to very small retail and service establishments in urban neighborhoods of high chronic unemployment help them provide more jobs for minorities and thus further the President's war on poverty? To provide at least a partial answer to this question, SBA's Philadelphia

Regional Office proposed a pilot loan program in 

Philadelphia County. 

Preliminary research by the Drexel Institute of 

Technology, Philadelphia, had shown that about half the retail and service businesses in the county had no access to bank or other credit. The institute, the city of Philadelphia, and the Fellowship Commission, which represents 500 churches, colleges, and labor organizations, then united their resources in a nonprofit "Small Business Opportunity Corporation." SBOC in turn was able to qualify for a $300,000 Area Redevelopment Administration grant for research into the problem, to be conducted chiefly by Drexel Institute.. 

The SBA Administrator was apprised of the problem by SBA's Philadelphia Regional Office and an SBOC committee. Aware that in this time of economic change no business can prosper without access to credit, he approved a pilot loan program. 

As readied for action at the 1963 yearend, the program called for loans of up to $6,000 to very small firms in the county at 4 percent interest with a maximum maturity of 6 years. Proceeds could be used to build up inventory, improve equipment, and, in some instances, augment working capital. 

Other major features of the program, as projected for inauguration early in 1964, included screening of applicants by SBOC and intensive SBA counseling of borrowers' with a view to enlarging their earnings and payrolls and establishing their credit with banks.

If the program proved feasible and effective in Philadelphia County neighborhoods of high unemployment, SBA planned later to apply it in impoverished sections of Boston, New York, Chicago, Detroit, Los Angeles, and Washington.

Fair Employment and 

Advisory Programs Besides implementing the SBA equality policy, the Special Assistant for Minority Groups represents SBA on the President's Committee on

Equal Employment Opportunity and on the 

White House Subcabinet Group on Civil Rights; 

he serves also as SBA's Employment Policy Officer.

In this last capacity, the Special Assistant reviewed SBA's Washington and field office personnel practices to assure complete compliance with the agency's policy of utilizing capabilities of minority employes to their fullest extent. 

The Special Assistant also has worked closely with the Special Assistant in charge of SBA's

Small Business Advisory Councils, providing him with names of minority businessmen and businesswomen qualified to serve on these bodies at the State, regional, and National level. (For information on these appointments, see sec. X.) 55

Section X Improving SBA Operations and Administration 

Organizational realinement and functional changes, designed to improve SBA's programs of assistance to small businesses and to increase efficiency of the agency's operations, were instituted during 1963. 

Major changes included creation of the position of Executive Administrator to assist the Administrator in planning, coordinating, and directing SBA programs; establishment of a position of 

Special Assistant for Minority Groups to assure a more equitable treatment for minority employees and minority individuals seeking SBA assistance; and establishment of an Office of Audits to carry out financial and program audits and investigative programs of the agency, functions formerly located in other organizational units.

Other changes brought about the consolidation of all loan appraisal functions under the Deputy 

Administrator for Financial Assistance and creation of an Office of Assistant Administrator for Administration to direct all activities relating to personnel, budget, fiscal operations, management analysis, and administrative services. 

New Plan Speeds Field Loan Processing and Closing 

During 1963 SBA developed a standardized plan for agency field offices to expedite loan processing and closing, and to provide improved service to the public. The plan was adopted following extensive analysis and evaluation of pilot operations which conclusively proved the system would produce appreciable savings in time, provide an improved workflow, and elimination of backtracking during loan processing. The plan also will reduce unnecessary loan conditions and legal requirements, and overcollateralization. A continuing analysis of the plan is being conducted to determine overall results in the field and to identify additional areas for improvement. 

Employee Training in 1963 Stresses Management Courses 

SBA gave major emphasis during the year to training its top and middle managers through general and specialized management training programs. 

Specific training included group management courses for 80 top executives, stressing human relations and communications; executive development seminars of 1-2 weeks in length for top and middle managers; specialized inservice courses on principles of management, effective writing and speaking; and wide participation by agency personnel in management conferences throughout the country.

Individual programs of training in specialized fields unique to SBA also have been undertaken by Washington and regional office personnel through correspondence and by participation in American Institute of Banking and similar courses. A series of seven regional appraisal conferences involving appraisers of all of SBA's regional offices also was held during the year. 

Improvements in Issuing of Delegations of Authority 

The number of SBA delegation documents was reduced from a total of 370 to 26 during 1963. 

This was accomplished by issuing one delegation document each to the Executive Administrator, Assistant Administrator, and Deputy Administrators, and one standard delegation document to all 

Regional Directors. 

These officials in turn issued one document redelegating authority to their staffs. This resulted in a reduction in the number of central office dele- 56

gations from 80 to 10, and an even greater reduction in the field where the number was reduced from 290 to 16.

The standardization and reduction in the number of delegation documents has resulted in: (a) a reduction in paperwork, reviews and followups required in processing of delegations; (b) maintaining uniform and up-to-date delegations in all regions with like authority for like positions; (c) more authority for field offices with resultant better service; and (d) removal of SBA internal management authorities from formal delegations published in the Federal Register. Estimated overall savings resulting from these actions is $34,800, apart from other intangible benefits. 

Resident Auditors Assigned to All Regional Offices 

Under the sharply increased emphasis on decentralized operations, SBA has assigned resident auditors to each of the agency's regional offices. 

The auditors will be under the authority of the 

Regional Director. 

Assigning resident auditors to each of the regions will enable these offices to more readily recognize and correct their own deficiencies without waiting for auditing by the centralized staff. 

Technical review and direction will remain at the

Headquarters level to assure the highest possible quality of professional audit, including assurance of resident auditor objectivity.

On-site reviews by the central audit staff will further underwrite adequate resident auditor coverage and will serve as a management review directly under the Administrator.

SBA Interagency Branch Helps Small Businesses 

SBA is directed to make studies of the effect of Federal laws, programs, and regulations on the competitive strength of small business and to make recommendations to other Federal agencies on the adjustment of their programs and regulations to meet the needs of the country's small firms. 

During 1963, SBA moved to coordinate, consolidate, and accelerate this function of the agency by establishing an Interagency Branch in the 

Office of the General Counsel. 

Typical of the service to small business through this division's action are the favorable decisions rendered by the Tariff Commission and the Internal Revenue Service. In the first case, integrated steel companies had sought an increase in duties on imported steel rods which would have placed more than 1,000 small firms in an uncompetitive position with a few large fabricators. 

SBA submitted a statement pointing out the effect of such a rise on small firms. The Commission denied the increase in duty.

In a second case, SBA objected to regulations proposed by Internal Revenue Service changing the tax treatment for stock options as being unfair to small firms. The final regulation, as adopted, eliminated the provision.

Work Volume Increased, Costs Cut By SBA's General Counsel's Offices 

Improvements and economies in legal operations were effected throughout the year by making better use of branch counsel, delegated authority,. and simplified procedures.

For the first time branch counsel were brought to area meetings in Washington, Chicago, and

Denver with regional attorneys. This resulted in improved loan liquidation and litigation procedures by increasing use of branch counsel at the local level, thus saving time and travel expenses of regional office attorneys.

Loan litigation was decentralized from the regional to the Miami, Nashville, and Houston branch offices, which also resulted in savings in both time and money, and better utilization of branch counsel. No personnel increase was involved. 

Ways and means to simplify legal procedures, make better use of delegated authority, and improve general operations resulted from the fall meeting of the Regional Counsel. Many offices now report they have been able to simplify their work and handle a growing volume of work with no increase in personnel.

The General Counsel's staff also has assisted the 

Area Redevelopment Administration in speeding up the processing of ARA loans, including the drafting of loan authorizations. 

First Cases Filed Under Investment Act 

During 1963 two matters in the investment company program required legal action and litigation was started in behalf of SBA in Federal district courts.

One action was brought under section 310 of the Small Business Investment Act of 1958 to 

57

enforce a subpena previously issued by the agency during the course of a formal investigation of a small business investment company. 

The other action sought relief under sections 308(d) and 311 of the act. The agency's complaint, naming a small business investment company and its officers as defendants, sought injunctive relief, appointment of a receiver, a determination of violations leading to revocation of the company's license, and judgment for repayment of the Federal funds previously loaned the company.

These two cases are the first litigation instituted by SBA under the Investment Act. 

Reviews Leading to

Savings on Publications 

During the year studies were made to determine if savings could be achieved through a reduction in printing requirements of some SBA publications, or by the elimination or consolidation of others.

Several thousand dollars in annual savings already have been made possible by removing 

SBA's Products List Circular from the "free list" and placing it on a subscription basis. In addition, savings resulted from a reduction in printing requirements and a simplification of methods used to gather and screen the material. 

Advisory Councils Expanded,

Aid SBA Program Development 

Throughout 1963 SBA placed strong emphasis on enlarging the volunteer membership of the agency's national, regional, and State small business advisory councils. At yearend, membership in the regional and State councils had reached about 1,000, including 150 women and representatives of minority groups as new members of the 

State councils. Membership in the National 

Advisory Council increased 11 to 64 during the year.

Council members, predominantly owners and managers of small businesses who serve without pay, evaluate existing and proposed SBA programs, recommend specific programs, point out general small business problems, and promote the welfare of small business through SBA programs and services. In keeping with Federal regulations, agency personnel serve as chairmen of the councils.

During 1963, particular emphasis was placed on the 56 State advisory councils. To increase their effectiveness and sharpen the focus of their deliberations, the State councils were divided into the following subcommittees: financial assistance, procurement assistance, management assistance, investment division, community development program, research and development, and legal. 

Councils first meet in subcommittees and then in full session to pass upon their resolutions. All recommendations are carefully weighed by SBA and a written report explaining the action taken is forwarded to the council. 

At the close of 1963, upon recommendation of the councils, meetings were scheduled quarterly instead of the semiyearly sessions of the prior year. To ease travel time and costs, councils in 

Texas and Hawaii meet in local subcouncil sessions.

The National Advisory Council met in Washington in mid-May to review current SBA programs and policies. Of particular note was the 

National Council's resolution in support of tax legislation. 58

Section XI 

Defining "Small Business"

The primary responsibility of the Small Business Administration is to provide assistance to those concerns which qualify as "small business." 

This authority is provided under the Small Business Act and the Small Business Investment Act. 

The Small Business Act defines a small business as one which is independently owned and operated, not dominant in its field of operation, and which further meets the specific size standards developed by the Small Business Administration. 

Since the American economy is under constant change, and new knowledge, experience, and information are obtained, SBA makes necessary revisions in its basic size standards and in procedures for determining the size of specific concerns. This flexibility in the size standards regulations enables the agency to modify its specific size standards on an industry-by-industry basis in keeping with the general economic changes and fluctuations in the relative size of companies within industries. During 1963, SBA made a number of such changes in size standards and procedures. Methods of implementing the agency's size programs are changed when needed.

SBA Adopts Size Standards for Manufacturing Concerns

During the year SBA adopted for procurement purposes an industry-by-industry size standard for 426 manufacturing concerns with classifica- 

Eions of 500, 750 or 1,000 employees depending on the structure of the specific industry. 

Prior to the adoption of the industry-by-induscry size standards, SBA had a general size standard of 500 employees. Concerns that had more chan 500 employees but less than 1,000 employees could apply to the agency for a small business certificate. Adoption of the industry-by-industry size standard for manufacturing concerns, long 734-160 0-64-5 recommended by both Congress and industry, has proven vastly superior to the former certificate program. 

Additional Standards Revised,

Other Revisions Are Proposed 

In addition to the adoption of industry-by-industry size standards for manufacturing concerns for purposes of Government procurements, SBA revised or proposed revision of several existing standards.

Services. The size standards for concerns bidding on Government service contracts were changed from a number-of-employees basis to an annual-receipts basis. SBA believes that, for the vast majority of service industries, annual receipts are a more appropriate "yardstick" than number of employees.

War Claims Act of 1948, as Amended. SBA adopted a size standard for a small concern for the purpose of receiving priority payments under the War Claims Act of 1948, as amended. The agency will certify a concern as a small business upon request from the Foreign Claims Settlement Commission of the United States. The size standard will be the same as SBA size standard for the purpose of financial assistance as of October 22, 1962, the date the legislation was enacted. 

Pamphlet Helps Firms Determine Size Requirements in Contracting 

SBA has published a pamphlet entitled "Qualifying as a Small Business for Government Contracts and Subcontracts" which materially assists industry, Government, and others in determining which firms meet the small-business-size requirements for specific Government prime contracts and subcontracts. 59

Section XII The Financial Assistance Program (business and disaster loans) under the Small Business Act, and the Investment and Development Company

Assistance Program under the Small Business Investment Act, are financed from a revolving fund. 

Statutory Authorization The SBA Revolving Fund 

Capital for this fund is provided by appropriations pursuant to authority contained in the Small Business Act, as amended. In authorizing such appropriations, the Congress has established maximum dollar amounts of loans and investments and commitments therefor which may be outstanding at any one time. Status of Revolving Fund 

The amount appropriated through December 31, 1963, as the capital investment of the Government in the revolving fund is $1,350 million. This capital investment, together with interest, fee, and miscellaneous income, and repayments on loans and debentures held, comprises the total available to cover the Small Business Administration's share of authorized loans and debenture purchases, administrative expenses incurred in making and servicing loans, and interest payments to the Treasury Department on the net amount expended from the fund.

A detailed statement of the charges and credits to the fund through December 31, 1963, is shown in appendix C. As will be observed, the cumulative total of Government funds invested in loans and debentures through December 31, 1963, amounted to $1,597 million (of which about $859 million was outstanding) and undisbursed commitments at that date amounted to almost $170 million. A total of $228 million from appropriations and receipts remained available for future use. These figures are exclusive of investments and commitments reported under the head of Re construction Finance Corporation Liquidation 

Program. 

Concerning this latter program, effective July 1, 1962, the remaining assets and liabilities of the 

Reconstruction Finance Corporation liquidation fund were transferred to the SBA revolving fund for liquidation purposes. This transfer had been approved as a part of the 1963 budgetary program of SBA, and permitted the elimination of separate accounting records and budget schedules for a relatively nominal amount of assets remaining out of those transferred to the agency for adminis tration and liquidation under the provisions of Reorganization Plan No. 2 of 1954 and Reorganiza tion Plan No. 1 of 1957. A summary statement covering the progress in liquidation of these RFC assets is shown in appendix D.

An accompanying table reflects a distribution of the income and expense amounts among the three program activities financed from the revolving fund. This distribution is based to the extent possible on accounting records, and on statistical records for the remainder. Comparative data are given for 1961 and 1962. 

In appraising the net loss produced by these program activities, several factors peculiar to SBA's operations as compared with those of a private financial institution must be taken into consideration. 

1. For example, by statute, the maximum interest rate on disaster loans (other than "displaced business" loans) is fixed at 3 percent, and the maximum interest rate on business loans is set at 512 percent. At the same time, in fiscal year 1964 

SBA is paying the Treasury Department interest on outstanding disbursements from the fund at rates of 334, 3%, and 4 percent (depending on the average maturity of the outstanding loans). In fiscal year 1963, interest paid to the Treasury was based on rates of 35%, 334, and 3% percent. 

2. Since SBA's primary purpose is to aid and assist small business, and lending is one of the tools

This frequently involves as much personnel costs as though the loans were made. Also, since SBA does not have previous credit files on applicants and many of them are located in cities and towns remote from SBA offices, the investigation of applications often involves more time and cost than a private lending institution would be willing to expend. 

3. Further, the average-size business loan is small, and the resulting income consequently is small in relation to the work involved in the processing, closing, and servicing functions.

(Comparable detailed analyses with respect to loans to small business investment companies and to State and local development companies, and purchases of SBIC debentures, were not made, since no losses had been realized at that date. Business Loans. Through June 30, 1963, SBA 

had incurred a loss on 611 business loans, and had charged off a total of $7,368,071 of unpaid principal. Further, the field staff had estimated a loss on 783 additional loans in the amount of $14,092,- 000, for a total actual and estimated loss of $21,- 

460,071. This represents 1.7 percent of loans disbursed through that date.

It is SBA's belief, however, based on previous analyses, that loans disbursed during the past 2 or 3 years have not matured to a point where estimates of losses are reliable. If it is assumed that the agency's loss experience on loans disbursed prior to July 1, 1960, is a reasonably accurate measure, a possible loss of 2.5 percent of total disbursements is indicated. Applying such a percentage to the total loans disbursed through June 30, 1963, SBA would suffer an ultimate loss of $32,171,000 on the SBA share of disbursements totaling $1,289,260,000.

A breakdown of these loss data, by type of loan, and comparative figures as of June 30, 1962, and June 30, 1961, are shown in an accompanying table. 

Disaster Loans. Principal charged off through

June 30, 1963, totaled $1,522,932 in connection with 373 loans. On 385 additional loans, the field staff estimated losses in the amount of $2 million, making a total of $3,522,932 of actual and estimated losses. This represents 2.3 percent of disaster loans disbursed through that date. 

SBA's experience on loans disbursed prior to 

July 1, 1960, indicates a possible loss of 3.63 percent of total disbursements. The SBA share of disbursements through June 30, 1962, totaled $151,- 

705,000. Application of the estimated 3.63 percent to this total would indicate an ultimate loss of $5,502,000.

Appendix E SBA Administrative Expenses by Activity I. Business loans: 

A. Interviews and counseling- 

B. Lending: 

II. Disaster loans: 

1. Loan making…. 

2. Loan administration and liquidation_.- 

Jan. 1-Dec. 31, 1963 

A. Loan making- B. Loan administration and liquidation. - 

III. Investment and development company assistance and supervision: Licensing, regulating, and examining small business investment companies, providing capital and lending funds to such companies; and lending funds to State and local development companies---- IV. Procurement assistance: Assisting small firms to obtain a greater proportion of total purchases and contracts for supplies and services for the Government: 

A. Joint set-asides (assisting small business in obtaining contracts for supplies and services in connection with civilian agency requirements and for mobilization and defense, including research and development contracts). 

B. Certificates of competency (certifying to Government procurement officers with respect to the competency, as to capacity and credit, of any small business concern or group of such concerns to perform a specific Government contract) - - C. Technical assistance (providing assistance on problems relating to procurement opportunities, including notification of proposed Government procurements) – – D. Subcontracting (assisting small business concerns to obtain subcontracting and developing and referring specific subcontracting opportunities, and providing guidance and technical counsel to small firms to enable them to find and participate in subcontract work)- - - V. Property sales assistance: Assisting small businesses to have opportunities to bid on and obtain a fair share of property sold by Government agencies--- VI. General technical assistance: Assisting small businesses by advising and counseling on production and other technical matters; by maintaining a clearinghouse for information concerning the managing and operation of small business enterprises; and by making engineering and industrial potential surveys, administering pools, and advising on Government specifications and related matters------ VII. Research and development and products assistance: Assisting small businesses to participate to an increased extent in Government-financed research and development; in obtaining the benefits of research and development work performed at Government expense; and in locating and developing new products and processes- VIII. Management assistance and research: Assisting small firms through management and publications programs; cosponsoring administrative management courses; providing management counseling; and administering contracts for research studies. 

IX. Liquidation of RFC loans: Liquidating disaster and business loans transferred to SBA pursuant to 

Reorganization Plans No. 2 of 1954 and No. 1 of 1957, respectively-- X. Contracting: Executing contracts between the Small Business Administration and other U.S. agencies having procurement authorities and arranging for performance by subcontracting to small business concerns..

Appendix G 

Business Loans Approved in Year 

Beginning on page 86 is a listing of the 4,975 business loans approved by SBA in 1963.

A "C" following the loan amount indicates that the loan was approved and subsequently canceled in full during the year. In addition, 274 loans totaling $16,361,758 which were approved in prior periods were canceled during 1963. 

In the case of participation loans (which category includes guaranteed loans beginning July 1, 1963), the interest rate listed is that applicable to the participating institution's share of the loan. By law, the rate on SBA's share is limited to a maximum of 52 percent. Where deferred participation and guaranteed loans carry a higher rate of interest, the maximum rate of 5½ percent on the SBA share does not apply until the agency has purchased its share of the loan from the participating institution. 85
